Cheerful? Well… yeah, I suppose I’ve had times where I felt pretty high on things, like I could see exactly what needed doing and everybody else was just dragging their feet. I’ll be joking around, feeling like I’m ten feet tall, like I’ve got the whole plan laid out.

Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. So yes, I guess there’s been some of that lately.

Well… I guess if you ask it that way, yes, but I don’t see it as “too much.” I’m just trying to keep everything from falling apart.

Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. Yes… my wife would say definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but once I start explaining what needs doing, it’s like the words just keep coming out.

I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. My family says I talk over them, too fast, like they can’t even get a word in to tell me to stop.\n\nI don’t mean to run them over like that. It just feels like if I don’t get it all said right then, nobody’s going to understand what needs doing.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you feel rested after much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. I’d have to think on that. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still not real sure, Doctor. Two weeks is a specific stretch, and I don’t keep a calendar for moods like that. I’ve had days where I’m down or quiet, sure, especially when I’m sitting around thinking too much, but nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know if I can say that.

My wife notices those things more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve been sitting in that chair too long,” or “You’re not talking much,” but then I get distracted fixing something in the garage or checking a noise in the furnace, and I don’t think about how long it’s been.

I mean, I sleep when I’m tired, same as anybody, but lately there’s just been a lot to keep straight. My father used to say if a job’s worth doing, it’s worth doing right, and that stuck with me.\n\nSo… less sleep? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping count.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually cheerful or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Cheerful? Well… yeah, I suppose I’ve had times where I felt pretty high on things, like I could see exactly what needed doing and everybody else was just dragging their feet. I’ll be joking around, feeling like I’m ten feet tall, like I’ve got the whole plan laid out.\n\nBut then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap at them. Not because I’m trying to be mean, it’s just… it feels like people need to either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of the way. Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. And at work, these younger fellows, they need someone to show them how the job’s supposed to be done. I’ve been around long enough to know when a thing’s being done halfway.\n\nSo yes, I’ve taken on a lot, but that’s because somebody has to. I feel like I’m the only one with enough drive to get things done properly.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and the new garage isn’t going to build itself right, you know. And church needs organizing—people mean well, but somebody has to keep things moving. Same at work, too. These younger fellows, they don’t always understand how the job ought to be done unless someone shows them. So I’ve been handling all that, and now everyone’s making it sound like it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I sleep when I’m tired, same as anybody, but lately there’s just been a lot to keep straight. The garage plans, the move, people asking me questions, and if I don’t stay on top of it, things get done halfway. My father used to say if a job’s worth doing, it’s worth doing right, and that stuck with me.\n\nSo… less sleep? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping count.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually cheerful or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Cheerful? Well… yeah, I suppose I’ve had times where I felt pretty high on things, like I could see exactly what needed doing and everybody else was just dragging their feet. I’ll be joking around, feeling like I’m ten feet tall, like I’ve got the whole plan laid out.\n\nBut then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap at them. Not because I’m trying to be mean, it’s just… it feels like people need to either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of the way. Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. So yes, I guess there’s been some of that lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been taking on more projects or activities than you normally do?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess if you ask it that way, yes, but I don’t see it as “too much.” I’m just trying to keep everything from falling apart.\n\nWe’ve got the move, and then the new garage has to be rebuilt right—not slapped together like some folks do things now. Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. And at work, these younger fellows, they need someone to show them how the job’s supposed to be done. I’ve been around long enough to know when a thing’s being done halfway.\n\nSo yes, I’ve taken on a lot, but that’s because somebody has to. My wife says I’m overdoing it, my family says the same, but to me it just feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of extra drive and confidence come in distinct periods when you’re noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does come in spells, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually a pretty steady fellow, or I try to be. I worked around machines most of my life—grinders, mixers, all that—and you can’t be flying off every minute or somebody gets hurt.\n\nBut lately there are times where I just feel like I know exactly how everything ought to go, and everybody needs to get on board with my plans or get out of my way. I’ll be joking, feeling ten feet tall, like nothing can touch me… and then my wife or my boy asks one little question and I snap at them.\n\nIt’s not how I mean to be. It just comes over me like people are slowing the whole thing down.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… my wife would say definitely. It just feels like if I don’t get it all said right then, nobody’s going to understand what needs doing.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you feel rested after much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. I’m not sure. My wife would probably have a better answer than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least a couple of weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. I’d have to think on that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.

At work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.

And at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. Money decisions, driving, the move we were talking about—things like that. They acted like I couldn’t be the one deciding anymore. Which stung, you know. I’ve handled plenty in my life. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.

We had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. But yes, it caused serious problems.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF30.1, F31.1, F60.5, F60.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m doing too much. My family, some folks at church, even the younger guys at work act like I’m supposed to slow down, but I don’t see it that way. I feel like I’m the only one with enough drive to get things done properly.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and the new garage isn’t going to build itself right, you know. And church needs organizing—people mean well, but somebody has to keep things moving. Same at work, too. These younger fellows, they don’t always understand how the job ought to be done unless someone shows them. So I’ve been handling all that, and now everyone’s making it sound like it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I sleep when I’m tired, same as anybody, but lately there’s just been a lot to keep straight. The garage plans, the move, people asking me questions, and if I don’t stay on top of it, things get done halfway. My father used to say if a job’s worth doing, it’s worth doing right, and that stuck with me.\n\nSo… less sleep? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping count.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually cheerful or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Cheerful? Well… yeah, I suppose I’ve had times where I felt pretty high on things, like I could see exactly what needed doing and everybody else was just dragging their feet. I’ll be joking around, feeling like I’m ten feet tall, like I’ve got the whole plan laid out.\n\nBut then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap at them. Not because I’m trying to be mean, it’s just… it feels like people need to either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of the way. Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. So yes, I guess there’s been some of that lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been taking on more projects or activities than you normally do?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess if you ask it that way, yes, but I don’t see it as “too much.” I’m just trying to keep everything from falling apart.\n\nWe’ve got the move, and then the new garage has to be rebuilt right—not slapped together like some folks do things now. Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. And at work, these younger fellows, they need someone to show them how the job’s supposed to be done. I’ve been around long enough to know when a thing’s being done halfway.\n\nSo yes, I’ve taken on a lot, but that’s because somebody has to. My wife says I’m overdoing it, my family says the same, but to me it just feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of extra drive and confidence come in distinct periods when you’re noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does come in spells, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually a pretty steady fellow, or I try to be. I worked around machines most of my life—grinders, mixers, all that—and you can’t be flying off every minute or somebody gets hurt.\n\nBut lately there are times where I just feel like I know exactly how everything ought to go, and everybody needs to get on board with my plans or get out of my way. I’ll be joking, feeling ten feet tall, like nothing can touch me… and then my wife or my boy asks one little question and I snap at them.\n\nIt’s not how I mean to be. It just comes over me like people are slowing the whole thing down.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… my wife would say definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but once I start explaining what needs doing, it’s like the words just keep coming out.\n\nI’ll be laying out the whole plan—what goes where, who needs to call who, how it ought to be done—and then I’m off on some other part of it, like how we used to do things at the shop when people actually listened the first time. My family says I talk over them, too fast, like they can’t even get a word in to tell me to stop.\n\nI don’t mean to run them over like that. It just feels like if I don’t get it all said right then, nobody’s going to understand what needs doing.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you feel rested after much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. Sleep’s a funny thing at my age anyway—sometimes you’re up because your shoulder aches, sometimes because the house makes a noise and you’re thinking about whether the furnace is acting up. Used to be when I was working shifts around those machines, you learned to get by on what you got, because the grinder didn’t care if you were tired.\n\nSo… during those spells, I may be up and moving, but whether I feel truly rested? I’m not sure. My wife would probably have a better answer than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least a couple of weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? I’m not sure what you mean by “had to.” I’ve been in hospitals plenty over the years for one thing or another—when you work around heavy equipment long enough, you see waiting rooms more than you’d like—but for these spells specifically… I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer.\n\nMy wife might remember it better. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. So… I’m not trying to dodge you, Doctor, I just don’t know how to answer that straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been admitted to a psychiatric hospital because of one of these high-energy spells?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. My wife would know better. She’s the one who remembers which place was which.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy spells, have they ever caused serious problems at home, work, or in church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.\n\nAt work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. Money decisions, driving, the move we were talking about—things like that. They acted like I couldn’t be the one deciding anymore. Which stung, you know. I’ve handled plenty in my life. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.\n\nWe had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I don’t remember sitting there seeing, you know, a person in the room that nobody else saw, or hearing a voice clear as you talking to me.

Sometimes I’d think I heard a sound in the house or out in the garage and I’d go check it, because with machinery work you get used to listening for things—bearings, belts, little changes in noise. My wife would say, “There’s nothing there,” but that’s not the same as seeing things, I don’t think.

So… no, not that I can say for sure. I don’t have a clear memory of anything like that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.1, F30.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m doing too much. My family, some folks at church, even the younger guys at work act like I’m supposed to slow down, but I don’t see it that way. I feel like I’m the only one with enough drive to get things done properly.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and the new garage isn’t going to build itself right, you know. And church needs organizing—people mean well, but somebody has to keep things moving. Same at work, too. These younger fellows, they don’t always understand how the job ought to be done unless someone shows them. So I’ve been handling all that, and now everyone’s making it sound like it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I sleep when I’m tired, same as anybody, but lately there’s just been a lot to keep straight. The garage plans, the move, people asking me questions, and if I don’t stay on top of it, things get done halfway. My father used to say if a job’s worth doing, it’s worth doing right, and that stuck with me.\n\nSo… less sleep? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping count.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually cheerful or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Cheerful? Well… yeah, I suppose I’ve had times where I felt pretty high on things, like I could see exactly what needed doing and everybody else was just dragging their feet. I’ll be joking around, feeling like I’m ten feet tall, like I’ve got the whole plan laid out.\n\nBut then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap at them. Not because I’m trying to be mean, it’s just… it feels like people need to either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of the way. Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. So yes, I guess there’s been some of that lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been taking on more projects or activities than you normally do?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess if you ask it that way, yes, but I don’t see it as “too much.” I’m just trying to keep everything from falling apart.\n\nWe’ve got the move, and then the new garage has to be rebuilt right—not slapped together like some folks do things now. Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. And at work, these younger fellows, they need someone to show them how the job’s supposed to be done. I’ve been around long enough to know when a thing’s being done halfway.\n\nSo yes, I’ve taken on a lot, but that’s because somebody has to. My wife says I’m overdoing it, my family says the same, but to me it just feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of extra drive and confidence come in distinct periods when you’re noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does come in spells, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually a pretty steady fellow, or I try to be. I worked around machines most of my life—grinders, mixers, all that—and you can’t be flying off every minute or somebody gets hurt.\n\nBut lately there are times where I just feel like I know exactly how everything ought to go, and everybody needs to get on board with my plans or get out of my way. I’ll be joking, feeling ten feet tall, like nothing can touch me… and then my wife or my boy asks one little question and I snap at them.\n\nIt’s not how I mean to be. It just comes over me like people are slowing the whole thing down.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… my wife would say definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but once I start explaining what needs doing, it’s like the words just keep coming out.\n\nI’ll be laying out the whole plan—what goes where, who needs to call who, how it ought to be done—and then I’m off on some other part of it, like how we used to do things at the shop when people actually listened the first time. My family says I talk over them, too fast, like they can’t even get a word in to tell me to stop.\n\nI don’t mean to run them over like that. It just feels like if I don’t get it all said right then, nobody’s going to understand what needs doing.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you feel rested after much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. Sleep’s a funny thing at my age anyway—sometimes you’re up because your shoulder aches, sometimes because the house makes a noise and you’re thinking about whether the furnace is acting up. Used to be when I was working shifts around those machines, you learned to get by on what you got, because the grinder didn’t care if you were tired.\n\nSo… during those spells, I may be up and moving, but whether I feel truly rested? I’m not sure. My wife would probably have a better answer than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least a couple of weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? I’m not sure what you mean by “had to.” I’ve been in hospitals plenty over the years for one thing or another—when you work around heavy equipment long enough, you see waiting rooms more than you’d like—but for these spells specifically… I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer.\n\nMy wife might remember it better. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. So… I’m not trying to dodge you, Doctor, I just don’t know how to answer that straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been admitted to a psychiatric hospital because of one of these high-energy spells?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Psychiatric hospital, specifically… that’s where I get a little foggy on the wording. I’ve been admitted places before, but whether it was because of one of those high-energy spells, I couldn’t say cleanly.\n\nMy wife would remember the paperwork better than I do. I remember things like the hallway lights being too bright, or whether they gave me coffee that tasted burnt. The actual reason they wrote down… I don’t want to tell you wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not real sure, Doctor. Two weeks is a specific stretch, and I don’t keep a calendar for moods like that. I’ve had days where I’m down or quiet, sure, especially when I’m sitting around thinking too much, but nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know if I can say that.\n\nMy wife notices those things more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve been sitting in that chair too long,” or “You’re not talking much,” but then I get distracted fixing something in the garage or checking a noise in the furnace, and I don’t think about how long it’s been.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, were you ever admitted to a psychiatric hospital?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it straight, Doctor, but I still can’t quite pin that down. “Admitted to a psychiatric hospital” sounds like something there’d be a form for, and I’m not the one who kept those forms.\n\nI remember being in medical buildings, locked doors in some places, nurses coming in and out, that kind of thing—but I don’t want to say yes to the psychiatric part if I’m mixing it up with something else. My wife would know better. She’s the one who remembers which place was which.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy spells, have they ever caused serious problems at home, work, or in church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.\n\nAt work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. Money decisions, driving, the move we were talking about—things like that. They acted like I couldn’t be the one deciding anymore. Which stung, you know. I’ve handled plenty in my life. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.\n\nWe had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. But yes, it caused serious problems.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, did you ever hear or see things that other people said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I don’t remember sitting there seeing, you know, a person in the room that nobody else saw, or hearing a voice clear as you talking to me.\n\nSometimes I’d think I heard a sound in the house or out in the garage and I’d go check it, because with machinery work you get used to listening for things—bearings, belts, little changes in noise. My wife would say, “There’s nothing there,” but that’s not the same as seeing things, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, not that I can say for sure. I don’t have a clear memory of anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? I’m not sure what you mean by “had to.” I’ve been in hospitals plenty over the years for one thing or another—when you work around heavy equipment long enough, you see waiting rooms more than you’d like—but for these spells specifically… I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer.\n\nMy wife might remember it better. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. So… I’m not trying to dodge you, Doctor, I just don’t know how to answer that straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been admitted to a psychiatric hospital because of one of these high-energy spells?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Psychiatric hospital, specifically… that’s where I get a little foggy on the wording. I’ve been admitted places before, but whether it was because of one of those high-energy spells, I couldn’t say cleanly.\n\nMy wife would remember the paperwork better than I do. I remember things like the hallway lights being too bright, or whether they gave me coffee that tasted burnt. The actual reason they wrote down… I don’t want to tell you wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not real sure, Doctor. Two weeks is a specific stretch, and I don’t keep a calendar for moods like that. I’ve had days where I’m down or quiet, sure, especially when I’m sitting around thinking too much, but nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know if I can say that.\n\nMy wife notices those things more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve been sitting in that chair too long,” or “You’re not talking much,” but then I get distracted fixing something in the garage or checking a noise in the furnace, and I don’t think about how long it’s been.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, were you ever admitted to a psychiatric hospital?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it straight, Doctor, but I still can’t quite pin that down. “Admitted to a psychiatric hospital” sounds like something there’d be a form for, and I’m not the one who kept those forms.\n\nI remember being in medical buildings, locked doors in some places, nurses coming in and out, that kind of thing—but I don’t want to say yes to the psychiatric part if I’m mixing it up with something else. My wife would know better. She’s the one who remembers which place was which.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy spells, have they ever caused serious problems at home, work, or in church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.\n\nAt work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. Money decisions, driving, the move we were talking about—things like that. They acted like I couldn’t be the one deciding anymore. Which stung, you know. I’ve handled plenty in my life. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.\n\nWe had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. But yes, it caused serious problems.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, did you ever hear or see things that other people said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I don’t remember sitting there seeing, you know, a person in the room that nobody else saw, or hearing a voice clear as you talking to me.\n\nSometimes I’d think I heard a sound in the house or out in the garage and I’d go check it, because with machinery work you get used to listening for things—bearings, belts, little changes in noise. My wife would say, “There’s nothing there,” but that’s not the same as seeing things, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, not that I can say for sure. I don’t have a clear memory of anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know that I can put a clean “yes” on that. I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, especially when you get older and people you knew are gone, or work isn’t what it used to be.

So… I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clear stretch in mind like that right now.

I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. So… I’m not trying to dodge you, Doctor, I just don’t know how to answer that straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been admitted to a psychiatric hospital because of one of these high-energy spells?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Psychiatric hospital, specifically… that’s where I get a little foggy on the wording. I’m not sure. I still like the things I like, I think… though some days I don’t get around to them.

Like, I used to enjoy tinkering with things in the garage, organizing tools, looking through old parts. But then you go out there and one drawer is a mess, or you can’t find the right wrench, and next thing you know you’re thinking about that one machine at work that always had a bad vibration in the housing—there was a trick to listening to it, you know. Younger guys didn’t always hear it.

So… lost interest in almost everything? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I can’t say I’ve noticed it in a clear way.

Not because I’m trying to be mean, it’s just… it feels like people need to either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of the way. Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. So yes, I guess there’s been some of that lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been taking on more projects or activities than you normally do?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess if you ask it that way, yes, but I don’t see it as “too much.” I’m just trying to keep everything from falling apart.\n\nWe’ve got the move, and then the new garage has to be rebuilt right—not slapped together like some folks do things now. Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. And at work, these younger fellows, they need someone to show them how the job’s supposed to be done. I’ve been around long enough to know when a thing’s being done halfway.\n\nSo yes, I’ve taken on a lot, but that’s because somebody has to. My wife says I’m overdoing it, my family says the same, but to me it just feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of extra drive and confidence come in distinct periods when you’re noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does come in spells, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually a pretty steady fellow, or I try to be. I worked around machines most of my life—grinders, mixers, all that—and you can’t be flying off every minute or somebody gets hurt.\n\nBut lately there are times where I just feel like I know exactly how everything ought to go, and everybody needs to get on board with my plans or get out of my way. I’ll be joking, feeling ten feet tall, like nothing can touch me… and then my wife or my boy asks one little question and I snap at them.\n\nIt’s not how I mean to be. It just comes over me like people are slowing the whole thing down.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… my wife would say definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but once I start explaining what needs doing, it’s like the words just keep coming out.\n\nI’ll be laying out the whole plan—what goes where, who needs to call who, how it ought to be done—and then I’m off on some other part of it, like how we used to do things at the shop when people actually listened the first time. My family says I talk over them, too fast, like they can’t even get a word in to tell me to stop.\n\nI don’t mean to run them over like that. It just feels like if I don’t get it all said right then, nobody’s going to understand what needs doing.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you feel rested after much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. Sleep’s a funny thing at my age anyway—sometimes you’re up because your shoulder aches, sometimes because the house makes a noise and you’re thinking about whether the furnace is acting up. Used to be when I was working shifts around those machines, you learned to get by on what you got, because the grinder didn’t care if you were tired.\n\nSo… during those spells, I may be up and moving, but whether I feel truly rested? I’m not sure. My wife would probably have a better answer than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least a couple of weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? I’m not sure what you mean by “had to.” I’ve been in hospitals plenty over the years for one thing or another—when you work around heavy equipment long enough, you see waiting rooms more than you’d like—but for these spells specifically… I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer.\n\nMy wife might remember it better. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. So… I’m not trying to dodge you, Doctor, I just don’t know how to answer that straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been admitted to a psychiatric hospital because of one of these high-energy spells?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Psychiatric hospital, specifically… that’s where I get a little foggy on the wording. I’ve been admitted places before, but whether it was because of one of those high-energy spells, I couldn’t say cleanly.\n\nMy wife would remember the paperwork better than I do. I remember things like the hallway lights being too bright, or whether they gave me coffee that tasted burnt. The actual reason they wrote down… I don’t want to tell you wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not real sure, Doctor. Two weeks is a specific stretch, and I don’t keep a calendar for moods like that. I’ve had days where I’m down or quiet, sure, especially when I’m sitting around thinking too much, but nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know if I can say that.\n\nMy wife notices those things more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve been sitting in that chair too long,” or “You’re not talking much,” but then I get distracted fixing something in the garage or checking a noise in the furnace, and I don’t think about how long it’s been.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, were you ever admitted to a psychiatric hospital?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it straight, Doctor, but I still can’t quite pin that down. “Admitted to a psychiatric hospital” sounds like something there’d be a form for, and I’m not the one who kept those forms.\n\nI remember being in medical buildings, locked doors in some places, nurses coming in and out, that kind of thing—but I don’t want to say yes to the psychiatric part if I’m mixing it up with something else. My wife would know better. She’s the one who remembers which place was which.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy spells, have they ever caused serious problems at home, work, or in church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.\n\nAt work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. Money decisions, driving, the move we were talking about—things like that. They acted like I couldn’t be the one deciding anymore. Which stung, you know. I’ve handled plenty in my life. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.\n\nWe had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. But yes, it caused serious problems.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, did you ever hear or see things that other people said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I don’t remember sitting there seeing, you know, a person in the room that nobody else saw, or hearing a voice clear as you talking to me.\n\nSometimes I’d think I heard a sound in the house or out in the garage and I’d go check it, because with machinery work you get used to listening for things—bearings, belts, little changes in noise. My wife would say, “There’s nothing there,” but that’s not the same as seeing things, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, not that I can say for sure. I don’t have a clear memory of anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can put a clean “yes” on that. I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, especially when you get older and people you knew are gone, or work isn’t what it used to be.\n\nBut “nearly every day for two weeks”… I’d have to think on that. My sense of time gets muddled sometimes. I’ll think something was a few days and my wife will say, no, that was longer, or the other way around. And then I start thinking about when I was still working around the grinders, because those weeks had a rhythm to them—you knew what day it was by what had to be cleaned or serviced.\n\nSo… I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clear stretch in mind like that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost interest in almost everything you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I still like the things I like, I think… though some days I don’t get around to them.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy tinkering with things in the garage, organizing tools, looking through old parts. But then you go out there and one drawer is a mess, or you can’t find the right wrench, and next thing you know you’re thinking about that one machine at work that always had a bad vibration in the housing—there was a trick to listening to it, you know. Younger guys didn’t always hear it.\n\nSo… lost interest in almost everything? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I can’t say I’ve noticed it in a clear way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates":["F31.1","F30.1"],"note":"","is_final":false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… that’s a little hard to separate. I can be stubborn, if that’s what you mean. I spent a lot of years around machines where if you knew a part was going bad, you knew it, even if somebody else said, “Nah, it’s fine.” And half the time they’d come back later and say I was right.

But clearly not true… I’m not sure. My wife might say I get fixed on something, like where something belongs in the garage or whether somebody moved a tool, and I’ll argue it more than I probably should. But I don’t have a clear example where everyone said, “That’s not real,” and I just wouldn’t budge.

So I guess… I’m not sure what counts there.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.1, F30.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m doing too much. My family, some folks at church, even the younger guys at work act like I’m supposed to slow down, but I don’t see it that way. I feel like I’m the only one with enough drive to get things done properly.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and the new garage isn’t going to build itself right, you know. And church needs organizing—people mean well, but somebody has to keep things moving. Same at work, too. These younger fellows, they don’t always understand how the job ought to be done unless someone shows them. So I’ve been handling all that, and now everyone’s making it sound like it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I sleep when I’m tired, same as anybody, but lately there’s just been a lot to keep straight. The garage plans, the move, people asking me questions, and if I don’t stay on top of it, things get done halfway. My father used to say if a job’s worth doing, it’s worth doing right, and that stuck with me.\n\nSo… less sleep? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping count.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually cheerful or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Cheerful? Well… yeah, I suppose I’ve had times where I felt pretty high on things, like I could see exactly what needed doing and everybody else was just dragging their feet. I’ll be joking around, feeling like I’m ten feet tall, like I’ve got the whole plan laid out.\n\nBut then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap at them. Not because I’m trying to be mean, it’s just… it feels like people need to either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of the way. Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. So yes, I guess there’s been some of that lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been taking on more projects or activities than you normally do?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess if you ask it that way, yes, but I don’t see it as “too much.” I’m just trying to keep everything from falling apart.\n\nWe’ve got the move, and then the new garage has to be rebuilt right—not slapped together like some folks do things now. Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. And at work, these younger fellows, they need someone to show them how the job’s supposed to be done. I’ve been around long enough to know when a thing’s being done halfway.\n\nSo yes, I’ve taken on a lot, but that’s because somebody has to. My wife says I’m overdoing it, my family says the same, but to me it just feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of extra drive and confidence come in distinct periods when you’re noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does come in spells, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually a pretty steady fellow, or I try to be. I worked around machines most of my life—grinders, mixers, all that—and you can’t be flying off every minute or somebody gets hurt.\n\nBut lately there are times where I just feel like I know exactly how everything ought to go, and everybody needs to get on board with my plans or get out of my way. I’ll be joking, feeling ten feet tall, like nothing can touch me… and then my wife or my boy asks one little question and I snap at them.\n\nIt’s not how I mean to be. It just comes over me like people are slowing the whole thing down.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… my wife would say definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but once I start explaining what needs doing, it’s like the words just keep coming out.\n\nI’ll be laying out the whole plan—what goes where, who needs to call who, how it ought to be done—and then I’m off on some other part of it, like how we used to do things at the shop when people actually listened the first time. My family says I talk over them, too fast, like they can’t even get a word in to tell me to stop.\n\nI don’t mean to run them over like that. It just feels like if I don’t get it all said right then, nobody’s going to understand what needs doing.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you feel rested after much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. Sleep’s a funny thing at my age anyway—sometimes you’re up because your shoulder aches, sometimes because the house makes a noise and you’re thinking about whether the furnace is acting up. Used to be when I was working shifts around those machines, you learned to get by on what you got, because the grinder didn’t care if you were tired.\n\nSo… during those spells, I may be up and moving, but whether I feel truly rested? I’m not sure. My wife would probably have a better answer than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least a couple of weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? I’m not sure what you mean by “had to.” I’ve been in hospitals plenty over the years for one thing or another—when you work around heavy equipment long enough, you see waiting rooms more than you’d like—but for these spells specifically… I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer.\n\nMy wife might remember it better. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. So… I’m not trying to dodge you, Doctor, I just don’t know how to answer that straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been admitted to a psychiatric hospital because of one of these high-energy spells?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Psychiatric hospital, specifically… that’s where I get a little foggy on the wording. I’ve been admitted places before, but whether it was because of one of those high-energy spells, I couldn’t say cleanly.\n\nMy wife would remember the paperwork better than I do. I remember things like the hallway lights being too bright, or whether they gave me coffee that tasted burnt. The actual reason they wrote down… I don’t want to tell you wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not real sure, Doctor. Two weeks is a specific stretch, and I don’t keep a calendar for moods like that. I’ve had days where I’m down or quiet, sure, especially when I’m sitting around thinking too much, but nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know if I can say that.\n\nMy wife notices those things more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve been sitting in that chair too long,” or “You’re not talking much,” but then I get distracted fixing something in the garage or checking a noise in the furnace, and I don’t think about how long it’s been.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, were you ever admitted to a psychiatric hospital?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it straight, Doctor, but I still can’t quite pin that down. “Admitted to a psychiatric hospital” sounds like something there’d be a form for, and I’m not the one who kept those forms.\n\nI remember being in medical buildings, locked doors in some places, nurses coming in and out, that kind of thing—but I don’t want to say yes to the psychiatric part if I’m mixing it up with something else. My wife would know better. She’s the one who remembers which place was which.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy spells, have they ever caused serious problems at home, work, or in church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.\n\nAt work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. Money decisions, driving, the move we were talking about—things like that. They acted like I couldn’t be the one deciding anymore. Which stung, you know. I’ve handled plenty in my life. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.\n\nWe had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. But yes, it caused serious problems.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, did you ever hear or see things that other people said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. She’s the one who remembers which place was which.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy spells, have they ever caused serious problems at home, work, or in church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.\n\nAt work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.\n\nWe had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. I don’t know that I can answer that straight. Locked unit… you mean where they don’t let you just walk out?

I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, especially when you get older and people you knew are gone, or work isn’t what it used to be.\n\nBut “nearly every day for two weeks”… I’d have to think on that. And then I start thinking about when I was still working around the grinders, because those weeks had a rhythm to them—you knew what day it was by what had to be cleaned or serviced.\n\nSo… I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clear stretch in mind like that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost interest in almost everything you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I still like the things I like, I think… though some days I don’t get around to them.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy tinkering with things in the garage, organizing tools, looking through old parts. But then you go out there and one drawer is a mess, or you can’t find the right wrench, and next thing you know you’re thinking about that one machine at work that always had a bad vibration in the housing—there was a trick to listening to it, you know. Younger guys didn’t always hear it.\n\nSo… lost interest in almost everything? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I can’t say I’ve noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: During one of those high-energy spells, did you ever become so convinced of an idea that other people said it was clearly not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a little hard to separate. I can be stubborn, if that’s what you mean. I spent a lot of years around machines where if you knew a part was going bad, you knew it, even if somebody else said, “Nah, it’s fine.” And half the time they’d come back later and say I was right.\n\nBut clearly not true… I’m not sure. My wife might say I get fixed on something, like where something belongs in the garage or whether somebody moved a tool, and I’ll argue it more than I probably should. But I don’t have a clear example where everyone said, “That’s not real,” and I just wouldn’t budge.\n\nSo I guess… I’m not sure what counts there.\n\nDoctor: Because of one of these high-energy spells, did you ever have to stay overnight in a locked psychiatric unit?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can answer that straight. Locked unit… you mean where they don’t let you just walk out?\n\nI’ve been in hospitals visiting people, and I never liked those doors that buzz before they open. Reminds me of the safety gates we had around some of the bigger machines—once they latch, you hear that sound and you know somebody’s in charge of when it opens. Funny how sounds stick with you.\n\nBut for me, because of one of those high-energy times… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of staying overnight in a place like that. I worked around machines most of my life—grinders, mixers, all that—and you can’t be flying off every minute or somebody gets hurt.\n\nBut lately there are times where I just feel like I know exactly how everything ought to go, and everybody needs to get on board with my plans or get out of my way. I don’t always notice it right away, but once I start explaining what needs doing, it’s like the words just keep coming out.\n\nI’ll be laying out the whole plan—what goes where, who needs to call who, how it ought to be done—and then I’m off on some other part of it, like how we used to do things at the shop when people actually listened the first time. My family says I talk over them, too fast, like they can’t even get a word in to tell me to stop.\n\nI don’t mean to run them over like that. Sleep’s a funny thing at my age anyway—sometimes you’re up because your shoulder aches, sometimes because the house makes a noise and you’re thinking about whether the furnace is acting up. My wife would probably have a better answer than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least a couple of weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? I’m not sure what you mean by “had to.” I’ve been in hospitals plenty over the years for one thing or another—when you work around heavy equipment long enough, you see waiting rooms more than you’d like—but for these spells specifically… I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer.\n\nMy wife might remember it better. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… money, yes, kind of. My wife took the checkbook, or I guess she started keeping it from me, and the cards too for a while. She said I wasn’t thinking straight with decisions, not just money but driving and the move we were trying to sort out.

It all got tangled together. I’d been pulled from work after a safety thing—machines don’t forgive you, you know, especially those grinders when somebody’s not watching the way they should. And after that my family started acting like I couldn’t be trusted to decide much of anything. I didn’t like it. Still don’t, really.

So, yes… they did step in with the money because they thought things had gotten out of hand.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.1, F30.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m doing too much. My family, some folks at church, even the younger guys at work act like I’m supposed to slow down, but I don’t see it that way. I feel like I’m the only one with enough drive to get things done properly.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and the new garage isn’t going to build itself right, you know. And church needs organizing—people mean well, but somebody has to keep things moving. Same at work, too. These younger fellows, they don’t always understand how the job ought to be done unless someone shows them. So I’ve been handling all that, and now everyone’s making it sound like it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I sleep when I’m tired, same as anybody, but lately there’s just been a lot to keep straight. The garage plans, the move, people asking me questions, and if I don’t stay on top of it, things get done halfway. My father used to say if a job’s worth doing, it’s worth doing right, and that stuck with me.\n\nSo… less sleep? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping count.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually cheerful or on top of the world?\n\nPatient: Cheerful? Well… yeah, I suppose I’ve had times where I felt pretty high on things, like I could see exactly what needed doing and everybody else was just dragging their feet. I’ll be joking around, feeling like I’m ten feet tall, like I’ve got the whole plan laid out.\n\nBut then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap at them. Not because I’m trying to be mean, it’s just… it feels like people need to either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of the way. Same thing happened years ago when we had to reorganize the shop floor—if folks don’t move when there’s work to be done, everything backs up. So yes, I guess there’s been some of that lately.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been taking on more projects or activities than you normally do?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess if you ask it that way, yes, but I don’t see it as “too much.” I’m just trying to keep everything from falling apart.\n\nWe’ve got the move, and then the new garage has to be rebuilt right—not slapped together like some folks do things now. Then there’s church, because people mean well but they wander off unless somebody keeps things organized. And at work, these younger fellows, they need someone to show them how the job’s supposed to be done. I’ve been around long enough to know when a thing’s being done halfway.\n\nSo yes, I’ve taken on a lot, but that’s because somebody has to. My wife says I’m overdoing it, my family says the same, but to me it just feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of extra drive and confidence come in distinct periods when you’re noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does come in spells, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually a pretty steady fellow, or I try to be. I worked around machines most of my life—grinders, mixers, all that—and you can’t be flying off every minute or somebody gets hurt.\n\nBut lately there are times where I just feel like I know exactly how everything ought to go, and everybody needs to get on board with my plans or get out of my way. I’ll be joking, feeling ten feet tall, like nothing can touch me… and then my wife or my boy asks one little question and I snap at them.\n\nIt’s not how I mean to be. It just comes over me like people are slowing the whole thing down.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… my wife would say definitely. I don’t always notice it right away, but once I start explaining what needs doing, it’s like the words just keep coming out.\n\nI’ll be laying out the whole plan—what goes where, who needs to call who, how it ought to be done—and then I’m off on some other part of it, like how we used to do things at the shop when people actually listened the first time. My family says I talk over them, too fast, like they can’t even get a word in to tell me to stop.\n\nI don’t mean to run them over like that. It just feels like if I don’t get it all said right then, nobody’s going to understand what needs doing.\n\nDoctor: During those spells, do you feel rested after much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say rested, exactly. Sleep’s a funny thing at my age anyway—sometimes you’re up because your shoulder aches, sometimes because the house makes a noise and you’re thinking about whether the furnace is acting up. Used to be when I was working shifts around those machines, you learned to get by on what you got, because the grinder didn’t care if you were tired.\n\nSo… during those spells, I may be up and moving, but whether I feel truly rested? I’m not sure. My wife would probably have a better answer than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least a couple of weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. I mean, everybody gets low now and again, especially when you get older and you’re not working like you used to. After I left the shop, there were plenty of days where I’d sit there and think about how the place ran, how the younger guys didn’t keep things as clean around the machines as we did, and that can get under your skin.\n\nBut a couple of weeks, most of the day, nearly every day… I don’t know. I’d have to think on that. My wife might say I’ve had stretches where I’m quieter or not myself, but I don’t keep track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: During one of these spells, have things ever gotten so severe that you had to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? I’m not sure what you mean by “had to.” I’ve been in hospitals plenty over the years for one thing or another—when you work around heavy equipment long enough, you see waiting rooms more than you’d like—but for these spells specifically… I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer.\n\nMy wife might remember it better. She keeps track of things like paperwork and dates. I tend to remember whether the nurses knew what they were doing, or if the bed rail was loose, things like that. So… I’m not trying to dodge you, Doctor, I just don’t know how to answer that straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been admitted to a psychiatric hospital because of one of these high-energy spells?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Psychiatric hospital, specifically… that’s where I get a little foggy on the wording. I’ve been admitted places before, but whether it was because of one of those high-energy spells, I couldn’t say cleanly.\n\nMy wife would remember the paperwork better than I do. I remember things like the hallway lights being too bright, or whether they gave me coffee that tasted burnt. The actual reason they wrote down… I don’t want to tell you wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m still not real sure, Doctor. Two weeks is a specific stretch, and I don’t keep a calendar for moods like that. I’ve had days where I’m down or quiet, sure, especially when I’m sitting around thinking too much, but nearly every day for two weeks… I don’t know if I can say that.\n\nMy wife notices those things more than I do. She’ll say, “You’ve been sitting in that chair too long,” or “You’re not talking much,” but then I get distracted fixing something in the garage or checking a noise in the furnace, and I don’t think about how long it’s been.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, were you ever admitted to a psychiatric hospital?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it straight, Doctor, but I still can’t quite pin that down. “Admitted to a psychiatric hospital” sounds like something there’d be a form for, and I’m not the one who kept those forms.\n\nI remember being in medical buildings, locked doors in some places, nurses coming in and out, that kind of thing—but I don’t want to say yes to the psychiatric part if I’m mixing it up with something else. My wife would know better. She’s the one who remembers which place was which.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy spells, have they ever caused serious problems at home, work, or in church?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it caused problems. I don’t like saying it that way, but it did.\n\nAt work, there was a safety incident and after that they pulled me from the job. In my line of work, with grinding and mixing and all that machinery, people don’t take chances—and I can’t blame them, though at the time I thought everybody was overreacting.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and the family stopped trusting me with certain things. Money decisions, driving, the move we were talking about—things like that. They acted like I couldn’t be the one deciding anymore. Which stung, you know. I’ve handled plenty in my life. But things had gotten out of hand, I guess.\n\nWe had this old filing cabinet at home where my wife kept papers, and she’d move things around because she didn’t want me making calls or signing something before she knew about it. I’d get mad about that, then later I’d forget what I was mad about and start looking for a receipt or a tape measure or something. But yes, it caused serious problems.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, did you ever hear or see things that other people said weren’t there?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I don’t remember sitting there seeing, you know, a person in the room that nobody else saw, or hearing a voice clear as you talking to me.\n\nSometimes I’d think I heard a sound in the house or out in the garage and I’d go check it, because with machinery work you get used to listening for things—bearings, belts, little changes in noise. My wife would say, “There’s nothing there,” but that’s not the same as seeing things, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, not that I can say for sure. I don’t have a clear memory of anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can put a clean “yes” on that. I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure—everybody has, especially when you get older and people you knew are gone, or work isn’t what it used to be.\n\nBut “nearly every day for two weeks”… I’d have to think on that. My sense of time gets muddled sometimes. I’ll think something was a few days and my wife will say, no, that was longer, or the other way around. And then I start thinking about when I was still working around the grinders, because those weeks had a rhythm to them—you knew what day it was by what had to be cleaned or serviced.\n\nSo… I can’t say for sure. I don’t have a clear stretch in mind like that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost interest in almost everything you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I still like the things I like, I think… though some days I don’t get around to them.\n\nLike, I used to enjoy tinkering with things in the garage, organizing tools, looking through old parts. But then you go out there and one drawer is a mess, or you can’t find the right wrench, and next thing you know you’re thinking about that one machine at work that always had a bad vibration in the housing—there was a trick to listening to it, you know. Younger guys didn’t always hear it.\n\nSo… lost interest in almost everything? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I can’t say I’ve noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: During one of those high-energy spells, did you ever become so convinced of an idea that other people said it was clearly not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… that’s a little hard to separate. I can be stubborn, if that’s what you mean. I spent a lot of years around machines where if you knew a part was going bad, you knew it, even if somebody else said, “Nah, it’s fine.” And half the time they’d come back later and say I was right.\n\nBut clearly not true… I’m not sure. My wife might say I get fixed on something, like where something belongs in the garage or whether somebody moved a tool, and I’ll argue it more than I probably should. But I don’t have a clear example where everyone said, “That’s not real,” and I just wouldn’t budge.\n\nSo I guess… I’m not sure what counts there.\n\nDoctor: Because of one of these high-energy spells, did you ever have to stay overnight in a locked psychiatric unit?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can answer that straight. Locked unit… you mean where they don’t let you just walk out?\n\nI’ve been in hospitals visiting people, and I never liked those doors that buzz before they open. Reminds me of the safety gates we had around some of the bigger machines—once they latch, you hear that sound and you know somebody’s in charge of when it opens. Funny how sounds stick with you.\n\nBut for me, because of one of those high-energy times… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of staying overnight in a place like that. My wife might remember better than I do, but I can’t give you a solid yes.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, did your family ever take away your access to money because they felt you couldn’t manage it safely?\n\nPatient: Well… money, yes, kind of. My wife took the checkbook, or I guess she started keeping it from me, and the cards too for a while. She said I wasn’t thinking straight with decisions, not just money but driving and the move we were trying to sort out.\n\nIt all got tangled together. I’d been pulled from work after a safety thing—machines don’t forgive you, you know, especially those grinders when somebody’s not watching the way they should. And after that my family started acting like I couldn’t be trusted to decide much of anything. I didn’t like it. Still don’t, really.\n\nSo, yes… they did step in with the money because they thought things had gotten out of hand.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t have a clear stretch in mind like that right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost interest in almost everything you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Overnight in a hospital… because of my mood or behavior? I’m not sure I’ve got that straight in my memory.

Locked unit… you mean where they don’t let you just walk out?\n\nI’ve been in hospitals visiting people, and I never liked those doors that buzz before they open. Reminds me of the safety gates we had around some of the bigger machines—once they latch, you hear that sound and you know somebody’s in charge of when it opens. Funny how sounds stick with you.\n\nBut for me, because of one of those high-energy times… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of staying overnight in a place like that. My wife might remember better than I do, but I can’t give you a solid yes.\n\nDoctor: During one of these high-energy spells, did your family ever take away your access to money because they felt you couldn’t manage it safely?\n\nPatient: Well… money, yes, kind of. My wife took the checkbook, or I guess she started keeping it from me, and the cards too for a while. She said I wasn’t thinking straight with decisions, not just money but driving and the move we were trying to sort out.\n\nIt all got tangled together. I’d been pulled from work after a safety thing—machines don’t forgive you, you know, especially those grinders when somebody’s not watching the way they should.
